And then the next day I’ll usually cut back — keep things lighter, skip the heavier stuff, maybe just coffee and something small — because I don’t want that higher number to stay there.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I do restrict because I don’t want to gain. I just usually frame it to myself as “getting back on track” or “not overdoing it,” but underneath that, I think it is because I don’t like the idea of the weight staying on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is actually too low, or do you see yourself as being at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people say I’m low. My friends have made comments, and obviously you’re asking me about it, so I get that there’s concern. I’m not saying everybody’s just making it up.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to really see it that way, because I’m still functioning. I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, doing the work. I’m not lying in bed all day or unable to show up. So part of me feels like… if it were really that bad, wouldn’t I be falling apart more? Wouldn’t I not be able to do what I do?\n\nSo when people act really worried, I kind of feel like they’re overreacting a little. Maybe I’m on the lean side, sure, but in my head I still think of myself as more normal than “too low.” Like, I can see why someone else might say I should put some weight on, but I don’t really look at myself and think, oh, this is dangerous or extreme. I mostly think I’m managing fine because I’m still showing up and getting my work done.\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it hasn’t stopped me from working, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting my painting jobs done. I show up, I finish the rooms, I deal with clients, I’m not calling out because of it or anything like that.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, I guess. Like if a client offers lunch, or if they want to take me out after a bigger job, I’ll sometimes find a reason not to go. I’ll say I’ve got another job to get to, or I already ate, or I need to pick something up from the paint store. Sometimes that’s true, but sometimes it’s really because I don’t want to be sitting there with them watching what I order or asking why I’m not eating much.\n\nSame with friends, too. If people want to meet up somewhere that’s all food-focused, I can get kind of uncomfortable ahead of time. Not always, but enough that I’ll avoid it sometimes. And part of it is the food, but part of it is I don’t want it to turn into a whole conversation about my weight or how I look. People make little comments, even if they think they’re being helpful, and then I’m stuck thinking about it for the rest of the night.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it interferes some. Not in a dramatic way where my whole life is falling apart, but I do plan around it, and I avoid certain meals or social stuff because I don’t want to deal with the attention or the comments.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives your decisions about what and how much to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to admit that, because I like to tell myself I’m just being sensible or keeping things balanced, but if I’m really looking at what happens, the fear of the number going up is a big part of it.\n\nLike, if I have a client dinner or something where I eat more than I planned, especially bread or something heavier, I’ll check the scale and if it’s even up a little bit, I get this tense feeling right away. Then it’s hard not to start adjusting everything the next day. I’ll think, okay, I need to keep it lighter today, I need to make sure that doesn’t stay, I shouldn’t add more on top of that.\n\nSo it definitely drives the decisions. Maybe not every single bite, but more than I probably want to admit. A lot of what I choose is based on not wanting that higher number to stick.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is overwhelming or dominating your thoughts most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but it can get really loud in my head, yeah. Especially if something happens that makes me feel like I’ve gone off what I planned.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner, or I eat bread or something heavier than usual, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, but then if the scale is up even a little bit after that, I get this tight, tense feeling almost immediately. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I go straight to, okay, I have to correct this. I need to cut back tomorrow, keep things lighter, make sure it doesn’t stick.\n\nSo it does end up dominating more than I want to admit, because even when I’m doing normal stuff — working, talking to people, trying to get through the day — part of me is still tracking it in the background. What did I eat, what will the scale say, do I need to make up for it. I try to act like I’m not that bothered, but if the number moves up, even a little, it can kind of take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low because you actually see yourself as being underweight when you look in the mirror, or is it more about the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I guess, but not in a clean way. The number matters a lot, but I don’t think it’s only the number. I’ll catch myself in a mirror at a job, or see a photo someone took of me standing next to one of my murals, and then I’m looking at my stomach or my face more than the actual work.\n\nLike, I’ll zoom in or kind of stare at it and think, does my face look sharp enough, does my stomach look flat enough, do I look… I don’t know, controlled, I guess. And if I don’t like what I see, it can honestly color the whole day. Even if the mural came out well, even if the client was happy, I’ll keep thinking about how I looked in that picture.\n\nSo it’s not exactly that I look in the mirror and calmly think, “I’m underweight.” It’s more like I’m judging whether I look right to myself. And “right” usually means sharper, leaner. If my face looks softer or my stomach doesn’t look the way I want, then I feel like something is wrong, even if the scale isn’t dramatically different.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight or do you think you look normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually look at myself and think, “I’m underweight,” if that makes sense. I think I more often think I look… normal, or not quite where I want to be. And I know that probably sounds strange, because other people might see something different, but when it’s me looking, I’m not seeing it in that detached way.\n\nWhat happens is I catch myself in a mirror, or especially in photos — like if someone takes a picture of me next to one of my murals — and instead of looking at the painting or the work, I’m immediately checking my stomach and my face. Like, does my stomach look flat enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look the way I’m supposed to look. And if I don’t think I do, it can kind of ruin the whole day for me.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t really see “underweight.” I see areas that don’t look right, or don’t look controlled enough. Even if maybe, logically, I can understand that I might be thinner than some people, when I’m actually looking at myself, I usually just feel like I look normal or like I could look sharper.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is so intense that it's always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “always” feels like a big word, because I can still work and have conversations and do things, but it’s there a lot. It’s like this background noise that gets louder if anything changes.\n\nThe clearest example is the scale. If I have a client dinner, or I eat something I normally wouldn’t — like bread, or something heavier — I’ll try to act normal about it, but the next morning, if the number is up even a little, I get tense right away. Like my whole body kind of tightens. And then I start thinking, okay, I need to pull back today, keep it lighter, make sure it doesn’t stay there.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s every single second, but it can take over pretty quickly. Especially when I feel like I’ve messed up or gone outside the plan. Then it’s hard not to think about what I need to do to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, because I’m not inside their heads, but I get the sense that it’s different from what I’m seeing.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror at work, or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I can’t just look at it normally. I’m not looking at the colors or the wall or whether the piece came out well first. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And then I sort of judge the whole day based on that, which sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what happens.\n\nOther people might say the mural looks great, or they might not be paying attention to my body at all, but I’ll be stuck on whether my face looked too soft in the picture, or whether my stomach looked wrong from that angle. So yeah, I think what I’m seeing is probably not the same thing other people are seeing. Or at least it has a much bigger meaning to me than it probably does to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you think it should, regardless of what the scale says?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the scale can say one thing, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, then it doesn’t feel like I’m where I should be.\n\nIt’s especially bad with photos. Like if I’m standing next to one of my murals and someone takes a picture, I should be looking at the work, or thinking about whether the colors came out right, but I don’t. I zoom in on myself, basically. I’m checking my stomach, my face, the angles. And if something looks off to me, it kind of overrides everything else. The whole day can start to feel bad because of that one image.\n\nSo yeah, I guess regardless of the number, I’m still judging it by what I see — or what I think I see. And if I don’t look sharp enough, it doesn’t feel acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image or perception of your weight is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s accurate, no. Or… maybe I should say I don’t trust that it’s accurate, because in the moment it feels completely real to me.\n\nLike when I catch myself in a mirror, especially unexpectedly, I don’t just see “me.” I immediately start checking things — my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft. And photos are even worse, especially if I’m standing next to one of my murals. I’ll have just finished this huge piece, something I should feel proud of, and instead of looking at the colors or the wall or the work, I’m staring at myself in the picture and deciding how the whole day went based on how my body looks in that one shot.\n\nIf my stomach looks wrong to me, or my face doesn’t look defined enough, then it feels like proof that something is off, even if someone else probably wouldn’t notice anything. I’ll still work, because I kind of have to, and because once I’m focused on the painting I can keep moving. But meals with clients, dinners with people, anything where there’s a chance people are watching what I eat or commenting on how I look… I tend to back away from that. So yes, it does stop me from doing some things I probably want or need to do, just not usually the actual work itself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your body image issues are closely tied to your fear of gaining weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / painting_worker / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"If the scale goes up even a little after a client dinner or something with bread, I get tense and start cutting back the next day so it does not stick.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think they’re tied together pretty tightly. I don’t know if I always separate them in my head, honestly. Like, the body image part is what I’m seeing in the mirror or in a photo, but the fear underneath it is that it means I’m gaining, or that if I don’t catch it right away it’s going to keep going.

It can be something really small, too. If I have to do a client dinner, or if there’s bread with the meal and I eat some of it, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, like I’m being normal and not making it awkward. But then later, if the scale is up even a little bit — even if I know logically that could be water or just normal fluctuation — I get really tense. It feels like, okay, now I have to do something about it before it “sticks,” I guess. That’s the word that comes into my head.

So the next day I’ll start cutting back, trying to make up for it. And then I’m checking my body more, because I’m worried the gain is showing somehow. So yes, it’s not just that I don’t like how I look in some vague way. It’s very connected to this fear that any little increase means I’m losing control, and I need to fix it quickly.

---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here mostly because of an insurance physical, honestly. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should get it checked out, or talk to someone about it. I don’t know, to me it didn’t seem like this big alarming thing — I’ve always kind of figured I’m just lean. I do painting work, so I’m up and down ladders all day, carrying stuff, working long hours, sometimes in heat or in awkward positions, so I just assumed that’s how my body is from the job.\n\nBut he made it sound like it was something I shouldn’t just brush off, and I guess that stuck with me enough that I came in. I mean, I don’t really look at myself and think “too low,” I think more like… wiry, lean, working-guy build. But apparently the number was low enough that he wanted me to follow up, so that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I sit around terrified of it or anything, but if I’m being honest, I do get kind of tense when the number goes up. Like if I’ve had a client dinner or I ate more bread than usual or something heavier, and then the scale is even a little higher after that, it sticks in my head.\n\nI’ll tell myself it’s probably just the meal or water or whatever, but I still feel this pressure like, okay, I need to rein it back in tomorrow so it doesn’t stay there. So I’ll cut back the next day, sort of compensate, I guess. It’s not like I’m trying to make some dramatic change, but I don’t like the feeling that it might “stick,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. I don’t always think of it as “limiting” in the moment, because it’s kind of just become my routine, but most days I keep it pretty light. Like I’ll have coffee in the morning — sometimes more than one, depending on the job — and then if I’m going from one paint job to another I might have a protein bar in the truck or between places. Something quick, something that doesn’t sit heavy.\n\nThen at night I’ll usually have a small dinner. Not a big plate or anything. Part of it is I’m working and moving around all day, and honestly a full meal just feels like it would slow me down. If I eat something too heavy at lunch, I feel like I’m dragging, like climbing ladders or bending and carrying stuff becomes harder. So I tell myself it’s practical, you know — coffee, maybe a bar, get through the day, then something small later. But, yeah, if you’re asking whether I keep the amount and type of food pretty restricted, I’d say most days I probably do.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict your food intake because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that directly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” because that sounds kind of dramatic when I put it that way. But when the scale goes up even a little, especially after something like a client dinner or if I ate bread or a heavier meal, I get tense about it.\n\nIt’s like I’ll notice the number and then it just sits in the back of my mind. I start thinking, okay, was that just the food, is it water, is it going to go back down, or is that actually sticking? And then the next day I’ll usually cut back — keep things lighter, skip the heavier stuff, maybe just coffee and something small — because I don’t want that higher number to stay there.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I do restrict because I don’t want to gain. I just usually frame it to myself as “getting back on track” or “not overdoing it,” but underneath that, I think it is because I don’t like the idea of the weight staying on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is actually too low, or do you see yourself as being at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people say I’m low. My friends have made comments, and obviously you’re asking me about it, so I get that there’s concern. I’m not saying everybody’s just making it up.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to really see it that way, because I’m still functioning. I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, doing the work. I’m not lying in bed all day or unable to show up. So part of me feels like… if it were really that bad, wouldn’t I be falling apart more? Wouldn’t I not be able to do what I do?\n\nSo when people act really worried, I kind of feel like they’re overreacting a little. Maybe I’m on the lean side, sure, but in my head I still think of myself as more normal than “too low.” Like, I can see why someone else might say I should put some weight on, but I don’t really look at myself and think, oh, this is dangerous or extreme. I mostly think I’m managing fine because I’m still showing up and getting my work done.\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it hasn’t stopped me from working, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting my painting jobs done. I show up, I finish the rooms, I deal with clients, I’m not calling out because of it or anything like that.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, I guess. Like if a client offers lunch, or if they want to take me out after a bigger job, I’ll sometimes find a reason not to go. I’ll say I’ve got another job to get to, or I already ate, or I need to pick something up from the paint store. Sometimes that’s true, but sometimes it’s really because I don’t want to be sitting there with them watching what I order or asking why I’m not eating much.\n\nSame with friends, too. If people want to meet up somewhere that’s all food-focused, I can get kind of uncomfortable ahead of time. Not always, but enough that I’ll avoid it sometimes. And part of it is the food, but part of it is I don’t want it to turn into a whole conversation about my weight or how I look. People make little comments, even if they think they’re being helpful, and then I’m stuck thinking about it for the rest of the night.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it interferes some. Not in a dramatic way where my whole life is falling apart, but I do plan around it, and I avoid certain meals or social stuff because I don’t want to deal with the attention or the comments.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives your decisions about what and how much to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to admit that, because I like to tell myself I’m just being sensible or keeping things balanced, but if I’m really looking at what happens, the fear of the number going up is a big part of it.\n\nLike, if I have a client dinner or something where I eat more than I planned, especially bread or something heavier, I’ll check the scale and if it’s even up a little bit, I get this tense feeling right away. Then it’s hard not to start adjusting everything the next day. I’ll think, okay, I need to keep it lighter today, I need to make sure that doesn’t stay, I shouldn’t add more on top of that.\n\nSo it definitely drives the decisions. Maybe not every single bite, but more than I probably want to admit. A lot of what I choose is based on not wanting that higher number to stick.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is overwhelming or dominating your thoughts most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but it can get really loud in my head, yeah. Especially if something happens that makes me feel like I’ve gone off what I planned.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner, or I eat bread or something heavier than usual, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, but then if the scale is up even a little bit after that, I get this tight, tense feeling almost immediately. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I go straight to, okay, I have to correct this. I need to cut back tomorrow, keep things lighter, make sure it doesn’t stick.\n\nSo it does end up dominating more than I want to admit, because even when I’m doing normal stuff — working, talking to people, trying to get through the day — part of me is still tracking it in the background. What did I eat, what will the scale say, do I need to make up for it. I try to act like I’m not that bothered, but if the number moves up, even a little, it can kind of take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low because you actually see yourself as being underweight when you look in the mirror, or is it more about the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I guess, but not in a clean way. The number matters a lot, but I don’t think it’s only the number. I’ll catch myself in a mirror at a job, or see a photo someone took of me standing next to one of my murals, and then I’m looking at my stomach or my face more than the actual work.\n\nLike, I’ll zoom in or kind of stare at it and think, does my face look sharp enough, does my stomach look flat enough, do I look… I don’t know, controlled, I guess. And if I don’t like what I see, it can honestly color the whole day. Even if the mural came out well, even if the client was happy, I’ll keep thinking about how I looked in that picture.\n\nSo it’s not exactly that I look in the mirror and calmly think, “I’m underweight.” It’s more like I’m judging whether I look right to myself. And “right” usually means sharper, leaner. If my face looks softer or my stomach doesn’t look the way I want, then I feel like something is wrong, even if the scale isn’t dramatically different.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight or do you think you look normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually look at myself and think, “I’m underweight,” if that makes sense. I think I more often think I look… normal, or not quite where I want to be. And I know that probably sounds strange, because other people might see something different, but when it’s me looking, I’m not seeing it in that detached way.\n\nWhat happens is I catch myself in a mirror, or especially in photos — like if someone takes a picture of me next to one of my murals — and instead of looking at the painting or the work, I’m immediately checking my stomach and my face. Like, does my stomach look flat enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look the way I’m supposed to look. And if I don’t think I do, it can kind of ruin the whole day for me.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t really see “underweight.” I see areas that don’t look right, or don’t look controlled enough. Even if maybe, logically, I can understand that I might be thinner than some people, when I’m actually looking at myself, I usually just feel like I look normal or like I could look sharper.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is so intense that it's always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “always” feels like a big word, because I can still work and have conversations and do things, but it’s there a lot. It’s like this background noise that gets louder if anything changes.\n\nThe clearest example is the scale. If I have a client dinner, or I eat something I normally wouldn’t — like bread, or something heavier — I’ll try to act normal about it, but the next morning, if the number is up even a little, I get tense right away. Like my whole body kind of tightens. And then I start thinking, okay, I need to pull back today, keep it lighter, make sure it doesn’t stay there.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s every single second, but it can take over pretty quickly. Especially when I feel like I’ve messed up or gone outside the plan. Then it’s hard not to think about what I need to do to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, because I’m not inside their heads, but I get the sense that it’s different from what I’m seeing.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror at work, or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I can’t just look at it normally. I’m not looking at the colors or the wall or whether the piece came out well first. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And then I sort of judge the whole day based on that, which sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what happens.\n\nOther people might say the mural looks great, or they might not be paying attention to my body at all, but I’ll be stuck on whether my face looked too soft in the picture, or whether my stomach looked wrong from that angle. So yeah, I think what I’m seeing is probably not the same thing other people are seeing. Or at least it has a much bigger meaning to me than it probably does to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you think it should, regardless of what the scale says?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the scale can say one thing, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, then it doesn’t feel like I’m where I should be.\n\nIt’s especially bad with photos. Like if I’m standing next to one of my murals and someone takes a picture, I should be looking at the work, or thinking about whether the colors came out right, but I don’t. I zoom in on myself, basically. I’m checking my stomach, my face, the angles. And if something looks off to me, it kind of overrides everything else. The whole day can start to feel bad because of that one image.\n\nSo yeah, I guess regardless of the number, I’m still judging it by what I see — or what I think I see. And if I don’t look sharp enough, it doesn’t feel acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image or perception of your weight is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s accurate, no. Or… maybe I should say I don’t trust that it’s accurate, because in the moment it feels completely real to me.\n\nLike when I catch myself in a mirror, especially unexpectedly, I don’t just see “me.” I immediately start checking things — my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft. And photos are even worse, especially if I’m standing next to one of my murals. I’ll have just finished this huge piece, something I should feel proud of, and instead of looking at the colors or the wall or the work, I’m staring at myself in the picture and deciding how the whole day went based on how my body looks in that one shot.\n\nIf my stomach looks wrong to me, or my face doesn’t look defined enough, then it feels like proof that something is off, even if someone else probably wouldn’t notice anything. So logically, yeah, I can see that my perception probably isn’t accurate. But emotionally, when I’m looking at it, it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight stop you from doing things you want or need to do most days?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’d say it affects things, yeah, though I’m still functioning, if that makes sense. Like, I’m still showing up to painting jobs, I’m still getting the work done, I’m not canceling jobs or leaving walls half-finished because of it.\n\nBut it does change what I’ll agree to around the edges. If a client says, “Oh, stay for lunch,” or wants to take me out after a job, I usually try to get out of it. I’ll say I have another estimate to get to, or I need to clean equipment, or I already ate. Sometimes it’s not even about the food itself at first — it’s that I don’t want the whole situation to turn into people looking at what I’m eating, or making some comment like, “That’s all you’re having?” or “You look thin,” or the opposite, or just anything about my body.\n\nAnd with social plans too, I avoid some of them if I know food is going to be the main thing. I’ll still work, because I kind of have to, and because once I’m focused on the painting I can keep moving. But meals with clients, dinners with people, anything where there’s a chance people are watching what I eat or commenting on how I look… I tend to back away from that. So yes, it does stop me from doing some things I probably want or need to do, just not usually the actual work itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image issues are closely tied to your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think they’re tied together pretty tightly. I don’t know if I always separate them in my head, honestly. Like, the body image part is what I’m seeing in the mirror or in a photo, but the fear underneath it is that it means I’m gaining, or that if I don’t catch it right away it’s going to keep going.\n\nIt can be something really small, too. If I have to do a client dinner, or if there’s bread with the meal and I eat some of it, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, like I’m being normal and not making it awkward. But then later, if the scale is up even a little bit — even if I know logically that could be water or just normal fluctuation — I get really tense. It feels like, okay, now I have to do something about it before it “sticks,” I guess. That’s the word that comes into my head.\n\nSo the next day I’ll start cutting back, trying to make up for it. And then I’m checking my body more, because I’m worried the gain is showing somehow. So yes, it’s not just that I don’t like how I look in some vague way. It’s very connected to this fear that any little increase means I’m losing control, and I need to fix it quickly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People make little comments, even if they think they’re being helpful, and then I’m stuck thinking about it for the rest of the night.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it interferes some. Not in a dramatic way where my whole life is falling apart, but I do plan around it, and I avoid certain meals or social stuff because I don’t want to deal with the attention or the comments.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives your decisions about what and how much to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to admit that, because I like to tell myself I’m just being sensible or keeping things balanced, but if I’m really looking at what happens, the fear of the number going up is a big part of it.\n\nLike, if I have a client dinner or something where I eat more than I planned, especially bread or something heavier, I’ll check the scale and if it’s even up a little bit, I get this tense feeling right away. Then it’s hard not to start adjusting everything the next day. I’ll think, okay, I need to keep it lighter today, I need to make sure that doesn’t stay, I shouldn’t add more on top of that.\n\nSo it definitely drives the decisions. Maybe not every single bite, but more than I probably want to admit. A lot of what I choose is based on not wanting that higher number to stick.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is overwhelming or dominating your thoughts most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but it can get really loud in my head, yeah. Especially if something happens that makes me feel like I’ve gone off what I planned.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner, or I eat bread or something heavier than usual, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, but then if the scale is up even a little bit after that, I get this tight, tense feeling almost immediately. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I go straight to, okay, I have to correct this. I need to cut back tomorrow, keep things lighter, make sure it doesn’t stick.\n\nSo it does end up dominating more than I want to admit, because even when I’m doing normal stuff — working, talking to people, trying to get through the day — part of me is still tracking it in the background. What did I eat, what will the scale say, do I need to make up for it. I try to act like I’m not that bothered, but if the number moves up, even a little, it can kind of take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low because you actually see yourself as being underweight when you look in the mirror, or is it more about the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I guess, but not in a clean way. The number matters a lot, but I don’t think it’s only the number. I’ll catch myself in a mirror at a job, or see a photo someone took of me standing next to one of my murals, and then I’m looking at my stomach or my face more than the actual work.\n\nLike, I’ll zoom in or kind of stare at it and think, does my face look sharp enough, does my stomach look flat enough, do I look… I don’t know, controlled, I guess. And if I don’t like what I see, it can honestly color the whole day. Even if the mural came out well, even if the client was happy, I’ll keep thinking about how I looked in that picture.\n\nSo it’s not exactly that I look in the mirror and calmly think, “I’m underweight.” It’s more like I’m judging whether I look right to myself. And “right” usually means sharper, leaner. If my face looks softer or my stomach doesn’t look the way I want, then I feel like something is wrong, even if the scale isn’t dramatically different.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight or do you think you look normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually look at myself and think, “I’m underweight,” if that makes sense. I think I more often think I look… normal, or not quite where I want to be. And I know that probably sounds strange, because other people might see something different, but when it’s me looking, I’m not seeing it in that detached way.\n\nWhat happens is I catch myself in a mirror, or especially in photos — like if someone takes a picture of me next to one of my murals — and instead of looking at the painting or the work, I’m immediately checking my stomach and my face. Like, does my stomach look flat enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look the way I’m supposed to look. And if I don’t think I do, it can kind of ruin the whole day for me.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t really see “underweight.” I see areas that don’t look right, or don’t look controlled enough. Even if maybe, logically, I can understand that I might be thinner than some people, when I’m actually looking at myself, I usually just feel like I look normal or like I could look sharper.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is so intense that it's always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “always” feels like a big word, because I can still work and have conversations and do things, but it’s there a lot. It’s like this background noise that gets louder if anything changes.\n\nThe clearest example is the scale. If I have a client dinner, or I eat something I normally wouldn’t — like bread, or something heavier — I’ll try to act normal about it, but the next morning, if the number is up even a little, I get tense right away. Like my whole body kind of tightens. And then I start thinking, okay, I need to pull back today, keep it lighter, make sure it doesn’t stay there.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s every single second, but it can take over pretty quickly. Especially when I feel like I’ve messed up or gone outside the plan. Then it’s hard not to think about what I need to do to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, because I’m not inside their heads, but I get the sense that it’s different from what I’m seeing.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror at work, or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I can’t just look at it normally. I’m not looking at the colors or the wall or whether the piece came out well first. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And then I sort of judge the whole day based on that, which sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what happens.\n\nOther people might say the mural looks great, or they might not be paying attention to my body at all, but I’ll be stuck on whether my face looked too soft in the picture, or whether my stomach looked wrong from that angle. So yeah, I think what I’m seeing is probably not the same thing other people are seeing. Or at least it has a much bigger meaning to me than it probably does to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you think it should, regardless of what the scale says?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the scale can say one thing, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, then it doesn’t feel like I’m where I should be.\n\nIt’s especially bad with photos. Like if I’m standing next to one of my murals and someone takes a picture, I should be looking at the work, or thinking about whether the colors came out right, but I don’t. I zoom in on myself, basically. I’m checking my stomach, my face, the angles. And if something looks off to me, it kind of overrides everything else. The whole day can start to feel bad because of that one image.\n\nSo yeah, I guess regardless of the number, I’m still judging it by what I see — or what I think I see. And if I don’t look sharp enough, it doesn’t feel acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image or perception of your weight is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s accurate, no. Or… maybe I should say I don’t trust that it’s accurate, because in the moment it feels completely real to me.\n\nLike when I catch myself in a mirror, especially unexpectedly, I don’t just see “me.” I immediately start checking things — my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft. And photos are even worse, especially if I’m standing next to one of my murals. I’ll have just finished this huge piece, something I should feel proud of, and instead of looking at the colors or the wall or the work, I’m staring at myself in the picture and deciding how the whole day went based on how my body looks in that one shot.\n\nIf my stomach looks wrong to me, or my face doesn’t look defined enough, then it feels like proof that something is off, even if someone else probably wouldn’t notice anything. So logically, yeah, I can see that my perception probably isn’t accurate. But emotionally, when I’m looking at it, it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight stop you from doing things you want or need to do most days?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’d say it affects things, yeah, though I’m still functioning, if that makes sense. Like, I’m still showing up to painting jobs, I’m still getting the work done, I’m not canceling jobs or leaving walls half-finished because of it.\n\nBut it does change what I’ll agree to around the edges. If a client says, “Oh, stay for lunch,” or wants to take me out after a job, I usually try to get out of it. I’ll say I have another estimate to get to, or I need to clean equipment, or I already ate. Sometimes it’s not even about the food itself at first — it’s that I don’t want the whole situation to turn into people looking at what I’m eating, or making some comment like, “That’s all you’re having?” or “You look thin,” or the opposite, or just anything about my body.\n\nAnd with social plans too, I avoid some of them if I know food is going to be the main thing. I’ll still work, because I kind of have to, and because once I’m focused on the painting I can keep moving. But meals with clients, dinners with people, anything where there’s a chance people are watching what I eat or commenting on how I look… I tend to back away from that. So yes, it does stop me from doing some things I probably want or need to do, just not usually the actual work itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image issues are closely tied to your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think they’re tied together pretty tightly. I don’t know if I always separate them in my head, honestly. Like, the body image part is what I’m seeing in the mirror or in a photo, but the fear underneath it is that it means I’m gaining, or that if I don’t catch it right away it’s going to keep going.\n\nIt can be something really small, too. If I have to do a client dinner, or if there’s bread with the meal and I eat some of it, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, like I’m being normal and not making it awkward. But then later, if the scale is up even a little bit — even if I know logically that could be water or just normal fluctuation — I get really tense. It feels like, okay, now I have to do something about it before it “sticks,” I guess. That’s the word that comes into my head.\n\nSo the next day I’ll start cutting back, trying to make up for it. And then I’m checking my body more, because I’m worried the gain is showing somehow. So yes, it’s not just that I don’t like how I look in some vague way. It’s very connected to this fear that any little increase means I’m losing control, and I need to fix it quickly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… not every single minute, but if you added it all up across the day, I think it’s probably more than an hour most days.

It’s not always like I’m sitting there deliberately thinking, “I’m afraid of gaining weight” for an hour straight. It’s more like it keeps popping back in. Especially after something like a client dinner, or if I ate bread or something I don’t usually plan for. The next morning, if the scale is even a little higher, I get this tight feeling, like I’ve done something wrong or like it’s starting to “stick.” Then I’m thinking about what I ate, whether it shows, what I need to cut back on that day, how careful I need to be.

And it can take up a lot of space because I’ll go back and forth with myself — part of me knows one small change on the scale doesn’t necessarily mean anything, but another part of me reacts like it’s urgent and I have to fix it immediately. So even while I’m working, it’s in the background. I’m painting, but I’m also thinking, okay, don’t eat too much later, keep it lighter tomorrow, make sure this doesn’t turn into real gain.

So, yeah… I’d say it does go over an hour, just broken up into pieces throughout the day.

So I’ll cut back the next day, sort of compensate, I guess. It’s not like I’m trying to make some dramatic change, but I don’t like the feeling that it might “stick,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often limit the amount or types of food you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. I don’t always think of it as “limiting” in the moment, because it’s kind of just become my routine, but most days I keep it pretty light. Like I’ll have coffee in the morning — sometimes more than one, depending on the job — and then if I’m going from one paint job to another I might have a protein bar in the truck or between places. Something quick, something that doesn’t sit heavy.\n\nThen at night I’ll usually have a small dinner. Not a big plate or anything. Part of it is I’m working and moving around all day, and honestly a full meal just feels like it would slow me down. If I eat something too heavy at lunch, I feel like I’m dragging, like climbing ladders or bending and carrying stuff becomes harder. So I tell myself it’s practical, you know — coffee, maybe a bar, get through the day, then something small later. But, yeah, if you’re asking whether I keep the amount and type of food pretty restricted, I’d say most days I probably do.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict your food intake because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that directly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” because that sounds kind of dramatic when I put it that way. But when the scale goes up even a little, especially after something like a client dinner or if I ate bread or a heavier meal, I get tense about it.\n\nIt’s like I’ll notice the number and then it just sits in the back of my mind. I start thinking, okay, was that just the food, is it water, is it going to go back down, or is that actually sticking? And then the next day I’ll usually cut back — keep things lighter, skip the heavier stuff, maybe just coffee and something small — because I don’t want that higher number to stay there.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess I do restrict because I don’t want to gain. I just usually frame it to myself as “getting back on track” or “not overdoing it,” but underneath that, I think it is because I don’t like the idea of the weight staying on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is actually too low, or do you see yourself as being at a normal weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know people say I’m low. My friends have made comments, and obviously you’re asking me about it, so I get that there’s concern. I’m not saying everybody’s just making it up.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to really see it that way, because I’m still functioning. I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, doing the work. I’m not lying in bed all day or unable to show up. So part of me feels like… if it were really that bad, wouldn’t I be falling apart more? Wouldn’t I not be able to do what I do?\n\nSo when people act really worried, I kind of feel like they’re overreacting a little. Maybe I’m on the lean side, sure, but in my head I still think of myself as more normal than “too low.” Like, I can see why someone else might say I should put some weight on, but I don’t really look at myself and think, oh, this is dangerous or extreme. I mostly think I’m managing fine because I’m still showing up and getting my work done.\n\nDoctor: Does worrying about gaining weight interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it hasn’t stopped me from working, if that’s what you mean. I’m still getting my painting jobs done. I show up, I finish the rooms, I deal with clients, I’m not calling out because of it or anything like that.\n\nBut it does affect things around the edges, I guess. Like if a client offers lunch, or if they want to take me out after a bigger job, I’ll sometimes find a reason not to go. I’ll say I’ve got another job to get to, or I already ate, or I need to pick something up from the paint store. Sometimes that’s true, but sometimes it’s really because I don’t want to be sitting there with them watching what I order or asking why I’m not eating much.\n\nSame with friends, too. If people want to meet up somewhere that’s all food-focused, I can get kind of uncomfortable ahead of time. Not always, but enough that I’ll avoid it sometimes. And part of it is the food, but part of it is I don’t want it to turn into a whole conversation about my weight or how I look. People make little comments, even if they think they’re being helpful, and then I’m stuck thinking about it for the rest of the night.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say it interferes some. Not in a dramatic way where my whole life is falling apart, but I do plan around it, and I avoid certain meals or social stuff because I don’t want to deal with the attention or the comments.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives your decisions about what and how much to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t always want to admit that, because I like to tell myself I’m just being sensible or keeping things balanced, but if I’m really looking at what happens, the fear of the number going up is a big part of it.\n\nLike, if I have a client dinner or something where I eat more than I planned, especially bread or something heavier, I’ll check the scale and if it’s even up a little bit, I get this tense feeling right away. Then it’s hard not to start adjusting everything the next day. I’ll think, okay, I need to keep it lighter today, I need to make sure that doesn’t stay, I shouldn’t add more on top of that.\n\nSo it definitely drives the decisions. Maybe not every single bite, but more than I probably want to admit. A lot of what I choose is based on not wanting that higher number to stick.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear of gaining weight is overwhelming or dominating your thoughts most of the time?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but it can get really loud in my head, yeah. Especially if something happens that makes me feel like I’ve gone off what I planned.\n\nLike if I have a client dinner, or I eat bread or something heavier than usual, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, but then if the scale is up even a little bit after that, I get this tight, tense feeling almost immediately. It’s not like I calmly think, “Oh, that’s normal fluctuation.” I go straight to, okay, I have to correct this. I need to cut back tomorrow, keep things lighter, make sure it doesn’t stick.\n\nSo it does end up dominating more than I want to admit, because even when I’m doing normal stuff — working, talking to people, trying to get through the day — part of me is still tracking it in the background. What did I eat, what will the scale say, do I need to make up for it. I try to act like I’m not that bothered, but if the number moves up, even a little, it can kind of take over.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is low because you actually see yourself as being underweight when you look in the mirror, or is it more about the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I guess, but not in a clean way. The number matters a lot, but I don’t think it’s only the number. I’ll catch myself in a mirror at a job, or see a photo someone took of me standing next to one of my murals, and then I’m looking at my stomach or my face more than the actual work.\n\nLike, I’ll zoom in or kind of stare at it and think, does my face look sharp enough, does my stomach look flat enough, do I look… I don’t know, controlled, I guess. And if I don’t like what I see, it can honestly color the whole day. Even if the mural came out well, even if the client was happy, I’ll keep thinking about how I looked in that picture.\n\nSo it’s not exactly that I look in the mirror and calmly think, “I’m underweight.” It’s more like I’m judging whether I look right to myself. And “right” usually means sharper, leaner. If my face looks softer or my stomach doesn’t look the way I want, then I feel like something is wrong, even if the scale isn’t dramatically different.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as being underweight or do you think you look normal?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually look at myself and think, “I’m underweight,” if that makes sense. I think I more often think I look… normal, or not quite where I want to be. And I know that probably sounds strange, because other people might see something different, but when it’s me looking, I’m not seeing it in that detached way.\n\nWhat happens is I catch myself in a mirror, or especially in photos — like if someone takes a picture of me next to one of my murals — and instead of looking at the painting or the work, I’m immediately checking my stomach and my face. Like, does my stomach look flat enough, does my face look sharp enough, do I look the way I’m supposed to look. And if I don’t think I do, it can kind of ruin the whole day for me.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I don’t really see “underweight.” I see areas that don’t look right, or don’t look controlled enough. Even if maybe, logically, I can understand that I might be thinner than some people, when I’m actually looking at myself, I usually just feel like I look normal or like I could look sharper.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is so intense that it's always on your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “always” feels like a big word, because I can still work and have conversations and do things, but it’s there a lot. It’s like this background noise that gets louder if anything changes.\n\nThe clearest example is the scale. If I have a client dinner, or I eat something I normally wouldn’t — like bread, or something heavier — I’ll try to act normal about it, but the next morning, if the number is up even a little, I get tense right away. Like my whole body kind of tightens. And then I start thinking, okay, I need to pull back today, keep it lighter, make sure it doesn’t stay there.\n\nSo I don’t know if it’s every single second, but it can take over pretty quickly. Especially when I feel like I’ve messed up or gone outside the plan. Then it’s hard not to think about what I need to do to fix it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, because I’m not inside their heads, but I get the sense that it’s different from what I’m seeing.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror at work, or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I can’t just look at it normally. I’m not looking at the colors or the wall or whether the piece came out well first. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And then I sort of judge the whole day based on that, which sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what happens.\n\nOther people might say the mural looks great, or they might not be paying attention to my body at all, but I’ll be stuck on whether my face looked too soft in the picture, or whether my stomach looked wrong from that angle. So yeah, I think what I’m seeing is probably not the same thing other people are seeing. Or at least it has a much bigger meaning to me than it probably does to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you think it should, regardless of what the scale says?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the scale can say one thing, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, then it doesn’t feel like I’m where I should be.\n\nIt’s especially bad with photos. Like if I’m standing next to one of my murals and someone takes a picture, I should be looking at the work, or thinking about whether the colors came out right, but I don’t. I zoom in on myself, basically. I’m checking my stomach, my face, the angles. And if something looks off to me, it kind of overrides everything else. The whole day can start to feel bad because of that one image.\n\nSo yeah, I guess regardless of the number, I’m still judging it by what I see — or what I think I see. And if I don’t look sharp enough, it doesn’t feel acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image or perception of your weight is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s accurate, no. Or… maybe I should say I don’t trust that it’s accurate, because in the moment it feels completely real to me.\n\nLike when I catch myself in a mirror, especially unexpectedly, I don’t just see “me.” I immediately start checking things — my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft. And photos are even worse, especially if I’m standing next to one of my murals. I’ll have just finished this huge piece, something I should feel proud of, and instead of looking at the colors or the wall or the work, I’m staring at myself in the picture and deciding how the whole day went based on how my body looks in that one shot.\n\nIf my stomach looks wrong to me, or my face doesn’t look defined enough, then it feels like proof that something is off, even if someone else probably wouldn’t notice anything. So logically, yeah, I can see that my perception probably isn’t accurate. But emotionally, when I’m looking at it, it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight stop you from doing things you want or need to do most days?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’d say it affects things, yeah, though I’m still functioning, if that makes sense. Like, I’m still showing up to painting jobs, I’m still getting the work done, I’m not canceling jobs or leaving walls half-finished because of it.\n\nBut it does change what I’ll agree to around the edges. If a client says, “Oh, stay for lunch,” or wants to take me out after a job, I usually try to get out of it. I’ll say I have another estimate to get to, or I need to clean equipment, or I already ate. Sometimes it’s not even about the food itself at first — it’s that I don’t want the whole situation to turn into people looking at what I’m eating, or making some comment like, “That’s all you’re having?” or “You look thin,” or the opposite, or just anything about my body.\n\nAnd with social plans too, I avoid some of them if I know food is going to be the main thing. I’ll still work, because I kind of have to, and because once I’m focused on the painting I can keep moving. But meals with clients, dinners with people, anything where there’s a chance people are watching what I eat or commenting on how I look… I tend to back away from that. So yes, it does stop me from doing some things I probably want or need to do, just not usually the actual work itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image issues are closely tied to your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think they’re tied together pretty tightly. I don’t know if I always separate them in my head, honestly. Like, the body image part is what I’m seeing in the mirror or in a photo, but the fear underneath it is that it means I’m gaining, or that if I don’t catch it right away it’s going to keep going.\n\nIt can be something really small, too. If I have to do a client dinner, or if there’s bread with the meal and I eat some of it, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, like I’m being normal and not making it awkward. But then later, if the scale is up even a little bit — even if I know logically that could be water or just normal fluctuation — I get really tense. It feels like, okay, now I have to do something about it before it “sticks,” I guess. That’s the word that comes into my head.\n\nSo the next day I’ll start cutting back, trying to make up for it. And then I’m checking my body more, because I’m worried the gain is showing somehow. So yes, it’s not just that I don’t like how I look in some vague way. It’s very connected to this fear that any little increase means I’m losing control, and I need to fix it quickly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or fear of gaining weight for more than an hour every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not every single minute, but if you added it all up across the day, I think it’s probably more than an hour most days.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m sitting there deliberately thinking, “I’m afraid of gaining weight” for an hour straight. It’s more like it keeps popping back in. Especially after something like a client dinner, or if I ate bread or something I don’t usually plan for. The next morning, if the scale is even a little higher, I get this tight feeling, like I’ve done something wrong or like it’s starting to “stick.” Then I’m thinking about what I ate, whether it shows, what I need to cut back on that day, how careful I need to be.\n\nAnd it can take up a lot of space because I’ll go back and forth with myself — part of me knows one small change on the scale doesn’t necessarily mean anything, but another part of me reacts like it’s urgent and I have to fix it immediately. So even while I’m working, it’s in the background. I’m painting, but I’m also thinking, okay, don’t eat too much later, keep it lighter tomorrow, make sure this doesn’t turn into real gain.\n\nSo, yeah… I’d say it does go over an hour, just broken up into pieces throughout the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I don’t know exactly what other people see, because I’m not inside their heads, but I get the sense that it’s different from what I’m seeing.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror at work, or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I can’t just look at it normally. I’m not looking at the colors or the wall or whether the piece came out well first. I go straight to my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough. And then I sort of judge the whole day based on that, which sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what happens.\n\nOther people might say the mural looks great, or they might not be paying attention to my body at all, but I’ll be stuck on whether my face looked too soft in the picture, or whether my stomach looked wrong from that angle. So yeah, I think what I’m seeing is probably not the same thing other people are seeing. Or at least it has a much bigger meaning to me than it probably does to anyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you think it should, regardless of what the scale says?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… the scale can say one thing, but if I look in the mirror and my stomach doesn’t look flat enough, or my face doesn’t look sharp enough, then it doesn’t feel like I’m where I should be.\n\nIt’s especially bad with photos. Like if I’m standing next to one of my murals and someone takes a picture, I should be looking at the work, or thinking about whether the colors came out right, but I don’t. I zoom in on myself, basically. I’m checking my stomach, my face, the angles. And if something looks off to me, it kind of overrides everything else. The whole day can start to feel bad because of that one image.\n\nSo yeah, I guess regardless of the number, I’m still judging it by what I see — or what I think I see. And if I don’t look sharp enough, it doesn’t feel acceptable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image or perception of your weight is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s accurate, no. Or… maybe I should say I don’t trust that it’s accurate, because in the moment it feels completely real to me.\n\nLike when I catch myself in a mirror, especially unexpectedly, I don’t just see “me.” I immediately start checking things — my stomach, my face, whether I look sharp enough or kind of soft. And photos are even worse, especially if I’m standing next to one of my murals. I’ll have just finished this huge piece, something I should feel proud of, and instead of looking at the colors or the wall or the work, I’m staring at myself in the picture and deciding how the whole day went based on how my body looks in that one shot.\n\nIf my stomach looks wrong to me, or my face doesn’t look defined enough, then it feels like proof that something is off, even if someone else probably wouldn’t notice anything. So logically, yeah, I can see that my perception probably isn’t accurate. But emotionally, when I’m looking at it, it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight stop you from doing things you want or need to do most days?\n\nPatient: Most days… I’d say it affects things, yeah, though I’m still functioning, if that makes sense. Like, I’m still showing up to painting jobs, I’m still getting the work done, I’m not canceling jobs or leaving walls half-finished because of it.\n\nBut it does change what I’ll agree to around the edges. If a client says, “Oh, stay for lunch,” or wants to take me out after a job, I usually try to get out of it. I’ll say I have another estimate to get to, or I need to clean equipment, or I already ate. Sometimes it’s not even about the food itself at first — it’s that I don’t want the whole situation to turn into people looking at what I’m eating, or making some comment like, “That’s all you’re having?” or “You look thin,” or the opposite, or just anything about my body.\n\nAnd with social plans too, I avoid some of them if I know food is going to be the main thing. I’ll still work, because I kind of have to, and because once I’m focused on the painting I can keep moving. But meals with clients, dinners with people, anything where there’s a chance people are watching what I eat or commenting on how I look… I tend to back away from that. So yes, it does stop me from doing some things I probably want or need to do, just not usually the actual work itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image issues are closely tied to your fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think they’re tied together pretty tightly. I don’t know if I always separate them in my head, honestly. Like, the body image part is what I’m seeing in the mirror or in a photo, but the fear underneath it is that it means I’m gaining, or that if I don’t catch it right away it’s going to keep going.\n\nIt can be something really small, too. If I have to do a client dinner, or if there’s bread with the meal and I eat some of it, I’ll tell myself it’s fine in the moment, like I’m being normal and not making it awkward. But then later, if the scale is up even a little bit — even if I know logically that could be water or just normal fluctuation — I get really tense. It feels like, okay, now I have to do something about it before it “sticks,” I guess. That’s the word that comes into my head.\n\nSo the next day I’ll start cutting back, trying to make up for it. And then I’m checking my body more, because I’m worried the gain is showing somehow. So yes, it’s not just that I don’t like how I look in some vague way. It’s very connected to this fear that any little increase means I’m losing control, and I need to fix it quickly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight or fear of gaining weight for more than an hour every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… not every single minute, but if you added it all up across the day, I think it’s probably more than an hour most days.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m sitting there deliberately thinking, “I’m afraid of gaining weight” for an hour straight. It’s more like it keeps popping back in. Especially after something like a client dinner, or if I ate bread or something I don’t usually plan for. The next morning, if the scale is even a little higher, I get this tight feeling, like I’ve done something wrong or like it’s starting to “stick.” Then I’m thinking about what I ate, whether it shows, what I need to cut back on that day, how careful I need to be.\n\nAnd it can take up a lot of space because I’ll go back and forth with myself — part of me knows one small change on the scale doesn’t necessarily mean anything, but another part of me reacts like it’s urgent and I have to fix it immediately. So even while I’m working, it’s in the background. I’m painting, but I’m also thinking, okay, don’t eat too much later, keep it lighter tomorrow, make sure this doesn’t turn into real gain.\n\nSo, yeah… I’d say it does go over an hour, just broken up into pieces throughout the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It’s almost like the fear of gaining weight doesn’t stay as a number in my head — it turns into what I think I’m seeing on my body.

Like, if I catch myself in a mirror, even just passing by one, I’ll check my stomach or my face without really meaning to. And if they don’t look… I don’t know, sharp enough, or defined enough, then my whole mood can shift. It can make me feel like the day is already kind of ruined, even if nothing else has actually happened.

It happens with photos too, especially if someone takes one of me standing next to a mural I finished. I should probably be looking at the work, or feeling proud of the job, but instead I’m zooming in on myself, looking at whether my face looks fuller or whether my stomach shows under my shirt. And then I sort of judge the whole day by that. If I look sharp enough, I can relax a little. If I don’t, then it feels like proof that I’ve gained or that I need to be more careful.

So yes, it’s very connected. It’s not just an abstract fear for me — it shows up in how I interpret what I look like.
